4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations

Exact Answer from Notebook:

Direct method: The principle of Gauss Elimination method is 
[
𝐴
∣
𝐵
]
[A∣B] reduced to the argument matrix of 
[

∣
𝐶

∣
𝐵
]
⟶
[
𝑈
∣
𝐶
]
[A∣B]⟶[U∣C]. Gauss Elimination method is a back substitution method.

Indirect Method (Iterative Method): This method is computation depends on the degree of accuracy required. It is a self-approximation method.

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method

Exact Answer from Notebook:

Rate of convergence:
The rate of convergence of Gauss Seidel is nearly two times that of Gauss Jacobi method.
(Also noted: each step in Gauss-Seidel uses the newly computed estimations directly in the same iteration).

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Exact Answer from Notebook:

It can be applied when the arguments (intervals) are unequally spaced as well as equally spaced.

It does not require constructing a difference table like Newton's forward/backward table.

4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations

Notebook Page: Page 17 & Page 30 (PDF Sources 26 & 39)

Exact Answer from Answer Key:

Direct method (Gauss Elimination method):
The principle of Gauss Elimination method is 
[
𝐴
∣
𝐵
]
[A∣B] reduced to the argument matrix of 
[
𝑈
∣
𝐶
]
[U∣C] where 
𝑈
U is upper triangular matrix 
[
𝐴
∣
𝐵
]
⟶
[
𝑈
∣
𝐶
]
[A∣B]⟶[U∣C]. Gauss Elimination method is a back substitution method.

Indirect Method (Iterative Method):
This method is computation depends on the degree of accuracy required. It is an self-approximation method.

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method

Notebook Page: Page 30 & Page 37 (PDF Sources 39 & 46)

Exact Answer from Answer Key:

Rate of convergence:
The rate of convergence of Gauss Seidel is nearly two times that of Gauss Jacobi method.
(In Gauss-Seidel, each newly computed variable is used immediately in the current iteration to compute the remaining variables).

7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods

Notebook Page: Page 17 & Page 25 (PDF Sources 26 & 34)

Exact Answer from Answer Key:

Gauss Elimination Method: The principle is 
[
𝐴
∣
𝐵
]
⟶
[
𝑈
∣
𝐶
]
[A∣B]⟶[U∣C] where

𝐴
∣
𝐵

[
𝐼
∣
𝐶
]
[A∣B]⟶[I∣C] where 
𝐼
I is identity matrix. It gives the solution directly without back substitution.

1. Define interpolation and extrapolation?

Notebook Page: Page 53 (PDF Source 62)

Exact Answer from Answer Key:

Interpolation: The value of 
𝑥
x lies between the interval 
𝑥
0
x
0
	​

 and 
𝑥
𝑛
x
n
	​

𝑦

𝑥

𝑥
0
x
0
	​

𝑥
𝑛
x
n
	​

 then the process of determining the value of 
𝑦
y is known as Extrapolation.

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Notebook Page: Page 53 & Page 58 (PDF Sources 62 & 67)

Exact Answer from Answer Key:

Lagrange's formula can be used for both unequally spaced and equally spaced values of 
𝑥
x.

It does not require calculating difference tables (forward or backward difference tables).

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method

Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 51, Question 18 (also Handwritten Notes, Page 30 / PDF Source 39)   
UNIT IV.pdf
+ 1

Solution:

Since the immediately calculated values in Gauss Seidel of the unknowns are used, the convergence in Gauss Seidel method will be more rapid than in Gauss Jacobi method. The rate of convergence of Gauss Seidel method is roughly twice that of Gauss Jacobi method.   
UNIT IV.pdf

7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods

Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 51–52, Question 24

Solution:

In Gauss Elimination method, the coefficient matrix reduced to upper triangular matrix and we get the solution by back substitution whereas in Gauss Jordan method, the coefficient matrix reduces to an unit or identity matrix and we get the solution without using back substitution.

UNIT-V: INTERPOLATION AND APPROXIMATION (PART A - 2 MARKS)

1. Define interpolation and extrapolation?

Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 75, Question 9 (also Handwritten Notes, Page 53 / PDF Source 62)   
UNIT V.pdf
+ 1

Solution:

The process of computing the value of a function inside the given range is called interpolation. The process of computing the value of a function outside the given range is called extrapolation.   
UNIT V.pdf

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 76, Question 14

Solution:

Newton's formula can be used only when the values of the independent variable 
𝑥
x are equally spaced. But Lagrange's interpolation formula can be used whether the values of the independent variable 
𝑥
x are equally spaced or not. Lagrange's formula can be used for inverse interpolation also, while Newton's formula cannot be used.

======================================================================
23MA1302 - LINEAR ALGEBRA AND NUMERICAL ANALYSIS
UNIT-IV & UNIT-V: PART A (2 MARKS) EXACT ANSWER KEY
======================================================================

----------------------------------------------------------------------
UNIT-IV: SOLUTION OF EQUATIONS AND EIGENVALUE PROBLEMS (PART A)
----------------------------------------------------------------------

1. State the order of convergence and convergence condition for Newton Raphson method.

Answer:
• The order of convergence of Newton Raphson method is 2 (quadratic).
• The convergence condition is: |f(x) · f''(x)| < [f'(x)]²


2. Establish iteration formula to find the reciprocal of a positive number N by Newton Raphson Method.

Answer:
Let x = 1/N  ⇒  1/x = N  ⇒  (1/x) - N = 0
(i.e.) f(x) = (1/x) - N
f(xₙ) = (1/xₙ) - N,   f'(xₙ) = -1 / xₙ²

By Newton Raphson method:
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ - [ f(xₙ) / f'(xₙ) ]
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ - [ ((1/xₙ) - N) / (-1/xₙ²) ]
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ + xₙ²((1/xₙ) - N)
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ + xₙ - N·xₙ²
xₙ₊₁ = 2xₙ - N·xₙ²   (or)   xₙ(2 - N·xₙ)


3. Find the Newton Raphson iterative formula for √a.

Answer:
Let x = √a  ⇒  x² = a
(i.e.) f(x) = x² - a  ⇒  f(xₙ) = xₙ² - a,   f'(xₙ) = 2xₙ

By Newton Raphson method:
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ - [ f(xₙ) / f'(xₙ) ]
xₙ₊₁ = xₙ - [ (xₙ² - a) / (2xₙ) ]
xₙ₊₁ = (2xₙ² - xₙ² + a) / (2xₙ)
xₙ₊₁ = (xₙ² + a) / (2xₙ)  =  (1/2) [ xₙ + (a / xₙ) ]


4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations.

Answer:
Direct Method:
  (i) We get exact solution.
  (ii) Simple, takes less time.
  (iii) Reduces [A|B] to upper triangular [U|C] or identity [I|C] matrix.

Iterative (Indirect) Method:
  (i) We get approximate solution (depends on degree of accuracy required).
  (ii) Time consuming and laborious (self-approximation method).


5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method?

Answer:
Since the immediately calculated values of the unknowns in Gauss Seidel are used in the same iteration, the convergence in Gauss Seidel method is more rapid than in Gauss Jacobi method. The rate of convergence of Gauss Seidel method is roughly twice (two times) that of Gauss Jacobi method.


6. By Gauss elimination method solve x + y = 2 and 2x + 3y = 5.

Answer:
The augmented matrix is:
[A, B] = [ 1   1  |  2 ]
         [ 2   3  |  5 ]

Apply row operation R₂ → R₂ - 2R₁:
       = [ 1   1  |  2 ]
         [ 0   1  |  1 ]

By back substitution:
x + y = 2  --- (1)
y = 1

Substitute y = 1 in (1):
x + 1 = 2  ⇒  x = 1
Hence, x = 1, y = 1.


7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods.

Answer:
In Gauss Elimination method, the coefficient matrix is reduced to an upper triangular matrix ([A|B] → [U|C]) and we get the solution by back substitution, whereas in Gauss Jordan method, the coefficient matrix reduces to a unit or identity matrix ([A|B] → [I|C]) and we get the solution directly without using back substitution.


8. Solve by using Gauss Jordan method: 2x + 3y = 8, x - y = 4.

Answer:
The augmented matrix is:
[A, B] = [ 2    3  |  8 ]
         [ 1   -1  |  4 ]

Apply R₁ ↔ R₂:
       = [ 1   -1  |  4 ]
         [ 2    3  |  8 ]

Apply R₂ → R₂ - 2R₁:
       = [ 1   -1  |  4 ]
         [ 0    5  |  0 ]

Apply R₂ → R₂ / 5:
       = [ 1   -1  |  4 ]
         [ 0    1  |  0 ]

Apply R₁ → R₁ + R₂:
       = [ 1    0  |  4 ]
         [ 0    1  |  0 ] = [I | C]

Hence, x = 4, y = 0.


9. State the sufficient condition for convergence of Gauss Seidel method.

Answer:
The coefficient matrix should be diagonally dominant. That is, the absolute value of the leading diagonal element in each row is greater than or equal to the sum of the absolute values of the other elements in that row:
|aᵢᵢ| ≥ ∑ |aᵢⱼ|  (for j ≠ i)


10. Find the dominant Eigen value of A = [ 1  2 ; 3  4 ] by power method.

Answer:
Assume initial vector X₀ = [ 1 ]
                           [ 1 ]

Iteration 1:
AX₀ = [ 1  2 ][ 1 ] = [ 3 ] = 7 [ 0.4286 ] = λ₁X₁
      [ 3  4 ][ 1 ]   [ 7 ]     [   1    ]

Iteration 2:
AX₁ = [ 1  2 ][ 0.4286 ] = [ 2.4286 ] = 5.2858 [ 0.4595 ] = λ₂X₂
      [ 3  4 ][   1    ]   [ 5.2858 ]          [   1    ]

Iteration 3:
AX₂ = [ 1  2 ][ 0.4595 ] = [ 2.4595 ] = 5.3785 [ 0.4573 ] = λ₃X₃
      [ 3  4 ][   1    ]   [ 5.3785 ]          [   1    ]

Iteration 4:
AX₃ = [ 1  2 ][ 0.4573 ] = [ 2.4573 ] = 5.3719 [ 0.4574 ] = λ₄X₄
      [ 3  4 ][   1    ]   [ 5.3719 ]          [   1    ]

Dominant Eigen value = 5.3722 (or 5.372) and Eigen vector = [ 0.4574, 1 ]ᵀ.


======================================================================
UNIT-V: INTERPOLATION AND APPROXIMATION (PART A - 2 MARKS)
======================================================================

1. Define interpolation and extrapolation?

Answer:
• Interpolation: The process of computing the value of a function inside the given range [x₀, xₙ] is called interpolation.
• Extrapolation: The process of computing the value of a function outside the given range [x₀, xₙ] is called extrapolation.


2. State Lagrange's interpolation formula.

Answer:
y = f(x) = [ (x - x₁)(x - x₂)...(x - xₙ) / (x₀ - x₁)(x₀ - x₂)...(x₀ - xₙ) ] · y₀
         + [ (x - x₀)(x - x₂)...(x - xₙ) / (x₁ - x₀)(x₁ - x₂)...(x₁ - xₙ) ] · y₁
         + ...
         + [ (x - x₀)(x - x₁)...(x - xₙ₋₁) / (xₙ - x₀)(xₙ - x₁)...(xₙ - xₙ₋₁) ] · yₙ


3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Answer:
Newton's formula can be used only when the values of the independent variable x are equally spaced. But Lagrange's interpolation formula can be used whether the values of the independent variable x are equally spaced or not. Lagrange's formula can also be used for inverse interpolation, while Newton's formula cannot be used.


4. Use Lagrange's formula to find the quadratic polynomial that takes these values:
x :  0   1   3
y :  0   1   0

Answer:
By Lagrange's formula for 3 points:
y = f(x) = [ (x - x₁)(x - x₂) / (x₀ - x₁)(x₀ - x₂) ] y₀
         + [ (x - x₀)(x - x₂) / (x₁ - x₀)(x₁ - x₂) ] y₁
         + [ (x - x₀)(x - x₁) / (x₂ - x₀)(x₂ - x₁) ] y₂

Substitute x₀ = 0, x₁ = 1, x₂ = 3 and y₀ = 0, y₁ = 1, y₂ = 0:
y = [ (x - 1)(x - 3) / (-1)(-3) ]·(0) + [ (x - 0)(x - 3) / (1)(-2) ]·(1) + [ (x - 0)(x - 1) / (3)(2) ]·(0)
y(x) = (x² - 3x) / (-2)  =  (-1/2)(x² - 3x)


5. Find the second divided differences with arguments a, b, c if f(x) = 1/x.

Answer:
The divided difference table is:
----------------------------------------------------------
  x  |  y = 1/x  |         Δy          |       Δ²y
----------------------------------------------------------
  a  |    1/a    |                     |
     |           |  (1/b - 1/a)/(b-a)  |
     |           |     = -1/(ab)       |  (-1/bc + 1/ab)/(c-a)
  b  |    1/b    |                     |     = 1/(abc)
     |           |  (1/c - 1/b)/(c-b)  |
     |           |     = -1/(bc)       |
  c  |    1/c    |                     |
----------------------------------------------------------
Hence, f(a, b, c) = 1 / (abc).


6. Given u₀ = 1, u₁ = 11, u₂ = 21, u₃ = 28, u₄ = 29. Find Δ⁴u₀.

Answer:
The forward difference table is:
-------------------------------------------------------
  x  |  y = uₓ  |  Δuₓ  |  Δ²uₓ  |  Δ³uₓ  |  Δ⁴uₓ
-------------------------------------------------------
  0  |     1    |       |        |        |
     |          |   10  |        |        |
  1  |    11    |       |    0   |        |
     |          |   10  |        |   -3   |
  2  |    21    |       |   -3   |        |    0
     |          |    7  |        |   -3   |
  3  |    28    |       |   -6   |        |
     |          |    1  |        |        |
  4  |    29    |       |        |        |
-------------------------------------------------------
Hence, Δ⁴u₀ = 0.


7. If f(x) = 1/x², find f(a,b) and f(a,b,c) by using divided differences.

Answer:
The divided difference table is:
-------------------------------------------------------------------------
  x  |  y = 1/x²  |         Δy = f(a,b)         |      Δ²y = f(a,b,c)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------
  a  |    1/a²    |                             |
     |            |  -(a + b) / (a²b²)          |
  b  |    1/b²    |                             |  (ab + bc + ca) / (a²b²c²)
     |            |  -(b + c) / (b²c²)          |
  c  |    1/c²    |                             |
-------------------------------------------------------------------------
Therefore:
f(a, b) = -(a + b) / (a²b²)
f(a, b, c) = (ab + bc + ca) / (a²b²c²)


8. State Newton's divided difference formula.

Answer:
y = y₀ + (x - x₀)Δy₀ + (x - x₀)(x - x₁)Δ²y₀ + (x - x₀)(x - x₁)(x - x₂)Δ³y₀ + ...
(or)
f(x) = f(x₀) + (x - x₀)f(x₀, x₁) + (x - x₀)(x - x₁)f(x₀, x₁, x₂) + ...


9. By using Newton's forward and backward difference formula, find the first derivative of the function f(x).

Answer:
1) From Newton's Forward Interpolation Formula (where u = (x - x₀)/h):
   dy/dx = (1/h) [ Δy₀ + ((2u - 1)/2)Δ²y₀ + ((3u² - 6u + 2)/6)Δ³y₀ + ((2u³ - 9u² + 11u - 3)/12)Δ⁴y₀ + ... ]
   At x = x₀ (u = 0):
   (dy/dx)ₓ₌ₓ₀ = (1/h) [ Δy₀ - (1/2)Δ²y₀ + (1/3)Δ³y₀ - (1/4)Δ⁴y₀ + ... ]

2) From Newton's Backward Interpolation Formula (where u = (x - xₙ)/h):
   dy/dx = (1/h) [ ∇yₙ + ((2u + 1)/2)∇²yₙ + ((3u² + 6u + 2)/6)∇³yₙ + ((2u³ + 9u² + 11u + 3)/12)∇⁴yₙ + ... ]
   At x = xₙ (u = 0):
   (dy/dx)ₓ₌ₓₙ = (1/h) [ ∇yₙ + (1/2)∇²yₙ + (1/3)∇³yₙ + (1/4)∇⁴yₙ + ... ]


10. Form the Newton divided difference table from the following data:
X :  0   1    2     5
Y :  2   3   12   147

Answer:
The Newton Divided Difference Table is:
----------------------------------------------------------------------
  x  |   y   |      Δy = f(xᵢ, xᵢ₊₁)     |     Δ²y     |     Δ³y
----------------------------------------------------------------------
  0  |   2   |                           |             |
     |       |  (3 - 2)/(1 - 0) = 1      |             |
  1  |   3   |                           | (9-1)/(2-0) |
     |       |  (12 - 3)/(2 - 1) = 9     |    = 4      | (9-4)/(5-0)
  2  |  12   |                           | (45-9)/(5-1)|    = 1
     |       |  (147 - 12)/(5 - 2) = 45  |    = 9      |
  5  |  147  |                           |             |
----------------------------------------------------------------------

<h1>23MA1302 – Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis<br>Unit-IV & Unit-V: Part A (2 Marks) Exact Answer Key</h1>

<h2>UNIT-IV: SOLUTION OF EQUATIONS AND EIGENVALUE PROBLEMS</h2>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">1. State the order of convergence and convergence condition for Newton Raphson method.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 48, Q3 / Handwritten Classwork Page 3</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The order of convergence of Newton Raphson method is <b>2 (quadratic)</b> and convergence condition is <b>|<i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) <i>f''</i>(<i>x</i>)| &lt; [<i>f'</i>(<i>x</i>)]<sup>2</sup></b>.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">2. Establish iteration formula to find the reciprocal of a positive number <i>N</i> by Newton Raphson Method.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 49, Q8 / Handwritten Classwork Page 16</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  Let <i>x</i> = 1/<i>N</i> &rArr; <i>N</i> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x</i></span></span> &rArr; <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x</i></span></span> &minus; <i>N</i> = 0<br>
  (i.e.) <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x</i></span></span> &minus; <i>N</i>, &nbsp; <i>f</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>) = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x<sub>n</sub></i></span></span> &minus; <i>N</i>, &nbsp; <i>f'</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>) = &minus;<span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup></span></span><br>
  By Newton Raphson method,<br>
  <i>x<sub>n+1</sub></i> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <span class="frac"><span><i>f</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span><span class="bottom"><i>f'</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span></span> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <span class="frac"><span>(1/<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>) &minus; <i>N</i></span><span class="bottom">&minus;1/<i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup></span></span> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i> + <i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup>(<span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>x<sub>n</sub></i></span></span> &minus; <i>N</i>) = <b><i>x<sub>n</sub></i>(2 &minus; <i>N x<sub>n</sub></i>)</b> &nbsp; (or <b>2<i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <i>N x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup></b>).</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">3. Find that Newton Raphson iterative formula for &radic;<i>a</i>.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 48–49, Q6 / Handwritten Classwork Page 12–13</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  Let <i>x</i> = &radic;<i>a</i> &rArr; <i>x</i><sup>2</sup> = <i>a</i><br>
  (i.e.) <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = <i>x</i><sup>2</sup> &minus; <i>a</i> &rArr; <i>f</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>) = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup> &minus; <i>a</i>, &nbsp; <i>f'</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>) = 2<i>x<sub>n</sub></i><br>
  By Newton Raphson method,<br>
  <i>x<sub>n+1</sub></i> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <span class="frac"><span><i>f</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span><span class="bottom"><i>f'</i>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span></span> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <span class="frac"><span><i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup> &minus; <i>a</i></span><span class="bottom">2<i>x<sub>n</sub></i></span></span> = <b><span class="frac"><span><i>x<sub>n</sub></i><sup>2</sup> + <i>a</i></span><span class="bottom">2<i>x<sub>n</sub></i></span></span> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">2</span></span>(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span><i>a</i></span><span class="bottom"><i>x<sub>n</sub></i></span></span>)</b>.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 51, Q22</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b>
  <table class="data-table">
    <tr><th>Direct Method</th><th>Iterative (Indirect) Method</th></tr>
    <tr><td>i) We get exact solution.</td><td>i) Approximate solution (depends on accuracy required).</td></tr>
    <tr><td>ii) Simple, takes less time.</td><td>ii) Time consuming, laborious.</td></tr>
  </table></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 51, Q18 / Handwritten Classwork Page 30</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  Since the immediately calculated values in Gauss Seidel of the unknowns are used, the convergence in Gauss Seidel method will be more rapid than in Gauss Jacobi method. The rate of convergence of Gauss Seidel method is roughly <b>twice</b> that of Gauss Jacobi method.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">6. By Gauss elimination method solve <i>x</i> + <i>y</i> = 2 and 2<i>x</i> + 3<i>y</i> = 5.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 52, Q29</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The augmented matrix is [<i>A</i>, <i>B</i>] = 
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>1</td><td>|</td><td>2</td></tr><tr><td>2</td><td>3</td><td>|</td><td>5</td></tr></table>
  &sim;
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>1</td><td>|</td><td>2</td></tr><tr><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>|</td><td>1</td></tr></table> (<i>R</i><sub>2</sub> = <i>R</i><sub>2</sub> &minus; 2<i>R</i><sub>1</sub>)<br>
  By back substitution, <i>x</i> + <i>y</i> = 2 --- (1) and <i>y</i> = 1.<br>
  (1) becomes <i>x</i> + 1 = 2 &rArr; <i>x</i> = 1. <b>Hence <i>x</i> = 1, <i>y</i> = 1.</b></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 51–52, Q24</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  In Gauss Elimination method, the coefficient matrix is reduced to an upper triangular matrix ([<i>A</i>|<i>B</i>] &rarr; [<i>U</i>|<i>C</i>]) and we get the solution by back substitution, whereas in Gauss Jordan method, the coefficient matrix reduces to a unit or identity matrix ([<i>A</i>|<i>B</i>] &rarr; [<i>I</i>|<i>C</i>]) and we get the solution without using back substitution.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">8. Solve by using Gauss Jordan method: 2<i>x</i> + 3<i>y</i> = 8, <i>x</i> &minus; <i>y</i> = 4.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 50, Q13 & Handwritten Classwork Page 25</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  [<i>A</i>, <i>B</i>] = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>2</td><td>3</td><td>|</td><td>8</td></tr><tr><td>1</td><td>&minus;1</td><td>|</td><td>4</td></tr></table>
  <span style="font-size:10pt;">(<i>R</i><sub>1</sub>&harr;<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>)</span> &sim;
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>&minus;1</td><td>|</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td>2</td><td>3</td><td>|</td><td>8</td></tr></table>
  <span style="font-size:10pt;">(<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>&rarr;<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>&minus;2<i>R</i><sub>1</sub>)</span> &sim;
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>&minus;1</td><td>|</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td>0</td><td>5</td><td>|</td><td>0</td></tr></table>
  <span style="font-size:10pt;">(<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>&rarr;<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>/5)</span> &sim;
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>&minus;1</td><td>|</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>|</td><td>0</td></tr></table>
  <span style="font-size:10pt;">(<i>R</i><sub>1</sub>&rarr;<i>R</i><sub>1</sub>+<i>R</i><sub>2</sub>)</span> &sim;
  <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>0</td><td>|</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>|</td><td>0</td></tr></table><br>
  <b>Hence <i>x</i> = 4, <i>y</i> = 0.</b></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">9. State the sufficient condition for convergence of Gauss Seidel method.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 50, Q14 & Q15 / Handwritten Classwork Page 30</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The coefficient matrix should be <b>diagonally dominant</b>. That is, the absolute value of the leading diagonal element is greater than or equal to the sum of the absolute values of the other elements in that row: |<i>a<sub>ii</sub></i>| &ge; &sum; |<i>a<sub>ij</sub></i>| (for <i>j</i> &ne; <i>i</i>).</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">10. Find the dominant Eigen value of <i>A</i> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td><td>2</td></tr><tr><td>3</td><td>4</td></tr></table> by power method.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT IV.pdf, Page 52, Q27 / Handwritten Classwork Page 44</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  Let <i>X</i><sub>0</sub> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>1</td></tr><tr><td>1</td></tr></table>. &nbsp;
  <i>AX</i><sub>0</sub> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>3</td></tr><tr><td>7</td></tr></table> = 7 <table class="matrix"><tr><td>0.4286</td></tr><tr><td>1</td></tr></table>; &nbsp;
  <i>AX</i><sub>1</sub> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>2.4286</td></tr><tr><td>5.2858</td></tr></table> = 5.2858 <table class="matrix"><tr><td>0.4595</td></tr><tr><td>1</td></tr></table>; &nbsp;
  <i>AX</i><sub>2</sub> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>2.4595</td></tr><tr><td>5.3785</td></tr></table> = 5.3785 <table class="matrix"><tr><td>0.4573</td></tr><tr><td>1</td></tr></table>; &nbsp;
  <i>AX</i><sub>3</sub> = <table class="matrix"><tr><td>2.4573</td></tr><tr><td>5.3719</td></tr></table> = 5.3719 <table class="matrix"><tr><td>0.4574</td></tr><tr><td>1</td></tr></table>.<br>
  <b>Dominant eigen value = 5.3722</b>.</div>
</div>

<h2>UNIT-V: INTERPOLATION AND APPROXIMATION</h2>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">1. Define interpolation and extrapolation?</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 75, Q9</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The process of computing the value of a function inside the given range is called <b>interpolation</b>. The process of computing the value of a function outside the given range is called <b>extrapolation</b>.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">2. State Lagrange's interpolation formula.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 75–76, Q13</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  <i>y</i> = <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = 
  <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)...(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)(<i>x</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)...(<i>x</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span></span> <i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + 
  <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)...(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x</i><sub>1</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i><sub>1</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)...(<i>x</i><sub>1</sub> &minus; <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)</span></span> <i>y</i><sub>1</sub> + ... + 
  <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)...(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x<sub>n&minus;1</sub></i>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)...(<i>x<sub>n</sub></i> &minus; <i>x<sub>n&minus;1</sub></i>)</span></span> <i>y<sub>n</sub></i></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 76, Q14</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  Newton's formula can be used only when the values of the independent variable <i>x</i> are equally spaced. But Lagrange's interpolation formula can be used whether the values of the independent variable <i>x</i> are equally spaced or not. Lagrange's formula can be used for inverse interpolation also, while Newton's formula cannot be used.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">4. Use Lagrange's formula to find the quadratic polynomial that takes these values: <i>x</i>: 0, 1, 3; <i>y</i>: 0, 1, 0.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 77–78, Q24</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  By Lagrange's formula:<br>
  <i>y</i> = <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)(<i>x</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)</span></span> <i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x</i><sub>1</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i><sub>1</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)</span></span> <i>y</i><sub>1</sub> + <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)</span><span class="bottom">(<i>x</i><sub>2</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i><sub>2</sub> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)</span></span> <i>y</i><sub>2</sub><br>
  <i>y</i> = <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; 1)(<i>x</i> &minus; 3)</span><span class="bottom">(0 &minus; 1)(0 &minus; 3)</span></span> (0) + <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; 0)(<i>x</i> &minus; 3)</span><span class="bottom">(1 &minus; 0)(1 &minus; 3)</span></span> (1) + <span class="frac"><span>(<i>x</i> &minus; 0)(<i>x</i> &minus; 1)</span><span class="bottom">(3 &minus; 0)(3 &minus; 1)</span></span> (0) &rArr; <b><i>y</i>(<i>x</i>) = <span class="frac"><span><i>x</i><sup>2</sup> &minus; 3<i>x</i></span><span class="bottom">&minus;2</span></span></b>.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">5. Find the second divided differences with arguments <i>a, b, c</i> if <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = 1/<i>x</i>.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 78–79, Q28</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The divided difference table is:
  <table class="data-table">
    <tr><th><i>x</i></th><th><i>y</i> = 1/<i>x</i></th><th>&Delta;<i>y</i></th><th>&Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>y</i></th></tr>
    <tr><td><i>a</i></td><td>1/<i>a</i></td><td rowspan="2">&minus;1/<i>ab</i></td><td rowspan="3"><b>1/<i>abc</i></b></td></tr>
    <tr><td><i>b</i></td><td>1/<i>b</i></td></tr>
    <tr><td><i>c</i></td><td>1/<i>c</i></td><td>&minus;1/<i>bc</i></td></tr>
  </table>
  <b>Hence <i>f</i>(<i>a, b, c</i>) = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>abc</i></span></span></b>.</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">6. Given <i>u</i><sub>0</sub> = 1, <i>u</i><sub>1</sub> = 11, <i>u</i><sub>2</sub> = 21, <i>u</i><sub>3</sub> = 28, <i>u</i><sub>4</sub> = 29. Find &Delta;<sup>4</sup><i>u</i><sub>0</sub>.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 74, Q7</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b>
  <table class="data-table">
    <tr><th><i>x</i></th><th><i>y</i> = <i>u<sub>x</sub></i></th><th>&Delta;<i>u<sub>x</sub></i></th><th>&Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>u<sub>x</sub></i></th><th>&Delta;<sup>3</sup><i>u<sub>x</sub></i></th><th>&Delta;<sup>4</sup><i>u<sub>x</sub></i></th></tr>
    <tr><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>10</td><td>0</td><td>&minus;3</td><td><b>0</b></td></tr>
    <tr><td>1</td><td>11</td><td>10</td><td>&minus;3</td><td>&minus;3</td><td></td></tr>
    <tr><td>2</td><td>21</td><td>7</td><td>&minus;6</td><td></td><td></td></tr>
    <tr><td>3</td><td>28</td><td>1</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr>
    <tr><td>4</td><td>29</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr>
  </table>
  <b>Hence &Delta;<sup>4</sup><i>u</i><sub>0</sub> = 0.</b></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">7. If <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>) = 1/<i>x</i><sup>2</sup>, find <i>f</i>(<i>a,b</i>) and <i>f</i>(<i>a,b,c</i>) by using divided differences.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 79, Q29</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  The divided difference table is:
  <table class="data-table">
    <tr><th><i>x</i></th><th><i>y</i> = 1/<i>x</i><sup>2</sup></th><th>&Delta;<i>y</i> = <i>f</i>(<i>a,b</i>)</th><th>&Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>y</i> = <i>f</i>(<i>a,b,c</i>)</th></tr>
    <tr><td><i>a</i></td><td>1/<i>a</i><sup>2</sup></td><td><b>&minus;(<i>a</i> + <i>b</i>) / <i>a</i><sup>2</sup><i>b</i><sup>2</sup></b></td><td><b>(<i>ab</i> + <i>bc</i> + <i>ca</i>) / <i>a</i><sup>2</sup><i>b</i><sup>2</sup><i>c</i><sup>2</sup></b></td></tr>
    <tr><td><i>b</i></td><td>1/<i>b</i><sup>2</sup></td><td>&minus;(<i>b</i> + <i>c</i>) / <i>b</i><sup>2</sup><i>c</i><sup>2</sup></td><td></td></tr>
    <tr><td><i>c</i></td><td>1/<i>c</i><sup>2</sup></td><td></td><td></td></tr>
  </table></div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">8. State Newton's divided difference formula.</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 75, Q12</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  <i>y</i> = <i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + (<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)&Delta;<i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + (<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)&Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + (<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>1</sub>)(<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>2</sub>)&Delta;<sup>3</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + ...</div>
</div>

<div class="q-block">
  <div class="q-title">9. By using Newton's forward and backward difference formula, find the first derivative of the function <i>f</i>(<i>x</i>).</div>
  <div class="source">Source: UNIT V.pdf, Page 79–80, Q31 & Q32</div>
  <div><b>Solution:</b><br>
  <b>Newton Forward Formula</b> (where <i>u</i> = (<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>)/<i>h</i>):<br>
  <span class="frac"><span><i>dy</i></span><span class="bottom"><i>dx</i></span></span> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>h</i></span></span> [ &Delta;<i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + <span class="frac"><span>2<i>u</i> &minus; 1</span><span class="bottom">2</span></span> &Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + <span class="frac"><span>3<i>u</i><sup>2</sup> &minus; 6<i>u</i> + 2</span><span class="bottom">6</span></span> &Delta;<sup>3</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + ... ] &nbsp; | &nbsp; At <i>x</i> = <i>x</i><sub>0</sub>: (<span class="frac"><span><i>dy</i></span><span class="bottom"><i>dx</i></span></span>)<sub><i>x=x</i><sub>0</sub></sub> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>h</i></span></span> [ &Delta;<i>y</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">2</span></span>&Delta;<sup>2</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">3</span></span>&Delta;<sup>3</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> &minus; <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">4</span></span>&Delta;<sup>4</sup><i>y</i><sub>0</sub> + ... ]<br><br>
  <b>Newton Backward Formula</b> (where <i>u</i> = (<i>x</i> &minus; <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>)/<i>h</i>):<br>
  <span class="frac"><span><i>dy</i></span><span class="bottom"><i>dx</i></span></span> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>h</i></span></span> [ &nabla;<i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span>2<i>u</i> + 1</span><span class="bottom">2</span></span> &nabla;<sup>2</sup><i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span>3<i>u</i><sup>2</sup> + 6<i>u</i> + 2</span><span class="bottom">6</span></span> &nabla;<sup>3</sup><i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + ... ] &nbsp; | &nbsp; At <i>x</i> = <i>x<sub>n</sub></i>: (<span class="frac"><span><i>dy</i></span><span class="bottom"><i>dx</i></span></span>)<sub><i>x=x<sub>n</sub></i></sub> = <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom"><i>h</i></span></span> [ &nabla;<i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">2</span></span>&nabla;<sup>2</sup><i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">3</span></span>&nabla;<sup>3</sup><i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + <span class="frac"><span>1</span><span class="bottom">4</span></span>&nabla;<sup>4</sup><i>y<sub>n</sub></i> + ... ]</div>
</div>

I understand your frustration. The reason matrices and formulas break when you paste into Word or export to PDF is that Word cannot handle multiline text blocks or raw code formatting without messing up the alignment.

Here is the entire content rewritten in regular, clean text format.

Zero code boxes

Zero HTML files to save

No multiline brackets that split across lines

You can highlight everything below, copy it directly, and paste it into Microsoft Word or Google Docs. It will stay in one piece.

23MA1302: LINEAR ALGEBRA AND NUMERICAL ANALYSIS
UNIT-IV & UNIT-V: PART A (2 MARKS) ANSWER KEY
UNIT-IV: SOLUTION OF EQUATIONS AND EIGENVALUE PROBLEMS

1. State the order of convergence and convergence condition for Newton Raphson method

Order of convergence: 2 (quadratic).   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Condition for convergence: |f(x) * f''(x)| < [f'(x)]².   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

2. Establish iteration formula to find the reciprocal of a positive number N by Newton Raphson Method

Let x = 1/N.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Then 1/x = N, which gives f(x) = (1/x) - N.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Differentiating with respect to x: f'(x) = -1/x².   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

By Newton-Raphson formula:

x_(n+1) = x_n - [ f(x_n) / f'(x_n) ]   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = x_n - [ ((1/x_n) - N) / (-1/x_n²) ]   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = x_n + x_n² * ((1/x_n) - N)   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = x_n + x_n - N * x_n²   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = 2x_n - N*x_n²  or  x_n(2 - N*x_n)   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

3. Find that Newton Raphson iterative formula for √a

Let x = √a, so x² = a.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Then f(x) = x² - a.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Differentiating: f'(x) = 2x.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

By Newton-Raphson formula:

x_(n+1) = x_n - [ f(x_n) / f'(x_n) ]   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = x_n - [ (x_n² - a) / (2x_n) ]   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = (2x_n² - x_n² + a) / (2x_n)   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

x_(n+1) = (x_n² + a) / (2x_n)  or  (1/2) * [ x_n + (a / x_n) ]   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations

Direct Methods (e.g., Gauss Elimination, Gauss-Jordan):   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

Gives exact solutions.

Simple and takes less computation time.

UNIT IV.pdf

Indirect / Iterative Methods (e.g., Gauss-Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel):

Gives approximate solutions that improve with each iteration.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Computation depends entirely on the degree of accuracy required.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method

In Gauss-Seidel method, as soon as a new value of an unknown is computed, it is immediately used in the same iteration to evaluate the remaining variables. Because of this, Gauss-Seidel converges much faster—its rate of convergence is roughly twice (2 times) that of Gauss-Jacobi.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

6. By Gauss elimination method solve x + y = 2 and 2x + 3y = 5

Augmented matrix [A|B]:

Row 1: [ 1,  1 | 2 ]   
UNIT IV.pdf

Row 2: [ 2,  3 | 5 ]   
UNIT IV.pdf

Perform row operation R2 -> R2 - 2*R1:

Row 1: [ 1,  1 | 2 ]   
UNIT IV.pdf

Row 2: [ 0,  1 | 1 ]   
UNIT IV.pdf

From Row 2: y = 1   
UNIT IV.pdf

From Row 1: x + y = 2  =>  x + 1 = 2  =>  x = 1   
UNIT IV.pdf

Solution: x = 1, y = 1   
UNIT IV.pdf

7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods

Gauss Elimination: Transforms the augmented matrix [A|B] into an upper triangular matrix [U|C]. The final solution must be obtained using back-substitution.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Gauss-Jordan: Transforms the augmented matrix [A|B] directly into an identity matrix [I|C]. The solution is obtained directly without back-substitution.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

8. Solve by using Gauss Jordan method: 2x + 3y = 8, x - y = 4

Augmented matrix [A|B]:

Row 1: [ 2,  3 | 8 ]

Row 2: [ 1, -1 | 4 ]

Swap R1 and R2 (R1 <-> R2):

Row 1: [ 1, -1 | 4 ]

Row 2: [ 2,  3 | 8 ]

R2 -> R2 - 2*R1:

Row 1: [ 1, -1 | 4 ]

Row 2: [ 0,  5 | 0 ]

R2 -> R2 / 5:

Row 1: [ 1, -1 | 4 ]

Row 2: [ 0,  1 | 0 ]

R1 -> R1 + R2:

Row 1: [ 1,  0 | 4 ]

Row 2: [ 0,  1 | 0 ]

Solution: x = 4, y = 0

9. State the sufficient condition for convergence of Gauss Seidel method

The coefficient matrix must be strictly diagonally dominant. That is, in every row, the absolute value of the leading diagonal element must be greater than or equal to the sum of the absolute values of all other coefficients in that row:   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

|a_ii| >= Sum of |a_ij| for all j != i   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

10. Find the dominant Eigen value of A = [1, 2 ; 3, 4] by power method

Given matrix A with Row 1 = [1, 2] and Row 2 = [3, 4].   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

Let initial trial vector be X_0 = [1, 1]^T.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

Iteration 1: A * X_0 = [3, 7]^T = 7 * [0.4286, 1]^T

Iteration 2: A * X_1 = [2.4286, 5.2858]^T = 5.2858 * [0.4595, 1]^T

Iteration 3: A * X_2 = [2.4595, 5.3785]^T = 5.3785 * [0.4573, 1]^T

Iteration 4: A * X_3 = [2.4573, 5.3719]^T = 5.3719 * [0.4574, 1]^T

The dominant eigenvalue is 5.3722 (or 5.372) and the corresponding eigenvector is [0.457, 1]^T.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

UNIT-V: INTERPOLATION AND APPROXIMATION

1. Define interpolation and extrapolation

Interpolation: The process of estimating the value of a dependent variable y for any given x that lies inside the range of the given tabular values [x_0, x_n].   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Extrapolation: The process of estimating the value of y for an x that lies outside the range [x_0, x_n].   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

2. State Lagrange's interpolation formula

y = f(x) =

[ (x - x_1)(x - x_2)...(x - x_n) / (x_0 - x_1)(x_0 - x_2)...(x_0 - x_n) ] * y_0

[ (x - x_0)(x - x_2)...(x - x_n) / (x_1 - x_0)(x_1 - x_2)...(x_1 - x_n) ] * y_1

...

[ (x - x_0)(x - x_1)...(x - x_(n-1)) / (x_n - x_0)(x_n - x_1)...(x_n - x_(n-1)) ] * y_n   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Lagrange's formula can be used whether the intervals between x-values are equally spaced or unequally spaced (Newton's forward/backward formula works only for equal intervals).   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Lagrange's formula does not require constructing difference tables and can also be used for inverse interpolation.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

4. Use Lagrange's formula to find the quadratic polynomial that takes these values: x = 0, 1, 3; y = 0, 1, 0

Given points: (x_0, y_0) = (0, 0), (x_1, y_1) = (1, 1), (x_2, y_2) = (3, 0).   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

Formula:

y = [ (x - x_1)(x - x_2) / (x_0 - x_1)(x_0 - x_2) ] * y_0

[ (x - x_0)(x - x_2) / (x_1 - x_0)(x_1 - x_2) ] * y_1

[ (x - x_0)(x - x_1) / (x_2 - x_0)(x_2 - x_1) ] * y_2   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Since y_0 = 0 and y_2 = 0, only the second term remains:   
UNIT V.pdf

y = [ (x - 0)(x - 3) / (1 - 0)(1 - 3) ] * (1)   
UNIT V.pdf

y = [ x(x - 3) / (1 * -2) ]   
UNIT V.pdf

y = (x² - 3x) / -2  or  (-1/2) * (x² - 3x)   
UNIT V.pdf

5. Find the second divided differences with arguments a, b, c if f(x) = 1/x

First divided differences:   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

f(a, b) = [ (1/b) - (1/a) ] / (b - a) = [ (a - b) / ab ] / (b - a) = -1 / (ab)

f(b, c) = [ (1/c) - (1/b) ] / (c - b) = [ (b - c) / bc ] / (c - b) = -1 / (bc)

lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

Second divided difference:   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

f(a, b, c) = [ f(b, c) - f(a, b) ] / (c - a)

f(a, b, c) = [ (-1/bc) - (-1/ab) ] / (c - a)

f(a, b, c) = [ (1/ab) - (1/bc) ] / (c - a) = [ (c - a) / (abc) ] / (c - a)

f(a, b, c) = 1 / (abc)   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

6. Given u_0 = 1, u_1 = 11, u_2 = 21, u_3 = 28, u_4 = 29. Find Δ⁴u_0

First differences (Δu):

Δu_0 = 11 - 1 = 10

Δu_1 = 21 - 11 = 10

Δu_2 = 28 - 21 = 7

Δu_3 = 29 - 28 = 1

Second differences (Δ²u):

Δ²u_0 = 10 - 10 = 0

Δ²u_1 = 7 - 10 = -3

Δ²u_2 = 1 - 7 = -6

Third differences (Δ³u):

Δ³u_0 = -3 - 0 = -3

Δ³u_1 = -6 - (-3) = -3

Fourth difference (Δ⁴u):

Δ⁴u_0 = -3 - (-3) = 0

UNIT V.pdf

Δ⁴u_0 = 0   
UNIT V.pdf

7. If f(x) = 1/x², find f(a,b) and f(a,b,c) by using divided differences

First divided difference:

f(a, b) = [ (1/b²) - (1/a²) ] / (b - a)

= [ (a² - b²) / (a²b²) ] / (b - a)

= [ (a - b)(a + b) / (a²b²) ] / (b - a)

= -(a + b) / (a²b²)

Second divided difference:

Similarly, f(b, c) = -(b + c) / (b²c²).

f(a, b, c) = [ f(b, c) - f(a, b) ] / (c - a)

= (ab + bc + ca) / (a²b²c²)

UNIT V.pdf

8. State Newton's divided difference formula

f(x) = f(x_0) + (x - x_0)*f(x_0, x_1) + (x - x_0)(x - x_1)*f(x_0, x_1, x_2) + (x - x_0)(x - x_1)(x - x_2)*f(x_0, x_1, x_2, x_3) + ...   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

(or y = y_0 + (x - x_0)Δy_0 + (x - x_0)(x - x_1)Δ²y_0 + ...)   
UNIT V.pdf

9. By using Newton's forward and backward difference formula, find the first derivative of the function f(x)

From Newton's Forward Formula (where u = (x - x_0)/h):

dy/dx = (1/h) * [ Δy_0 + ((2u - 1)/2)*Δ²y_0 + ((3u² - 6u + 2)/6)*Δ³y_0 + ... ]

At the starting point x = x_0 (where u = 0):

(dy/dx) at x_0 = (1/h) * [ Δy_0 - (1/2)*Δ²y_0 + (1/3)*Δ³y_0 - (1/4)*Δ⁴y_0 + ... ]

From Newton's Backward Formula (where u = (x - x_n)/h):

dy/dx = (1/h) * [ ∇y_n + ((2u + 1)/2)∇²y_n + ((3u² + 6u + 2)/6)∇³y_n + ... ]

At the end point x = x_n (where u = 0):

(dy/dx) at x_n = (1/h) * [ ∇y_n + (1/2)∇²y_n + (1/3)∇³y_n + (1/4)*∇⁴y_n + ... ]

UNIT V.pdf

10. Form the Newton divided difference table from the following data: x = 0, 1, 2, 5; y = 2, 3, 12, 147
   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

First Divided Differences (Δy):

Between x=0 and x=1: (3 - 2) / (1 - 0) = 1

Between x=1 and x=2: (12 - 3) / (2 - 1) = 9

Between x=2 and x=5: (147 - 12) / (5 - 2) = 135 / 3 = 45

Second Divided Differences (Δ²y):

From (1, 9): (9 - 1) / (2 - 0) = 8 / 2 = 4

From (9, 45): (45 - 9) / (5 - 1) = 36 / 4 = 9

Third Divided Difference (Δ³y):

From (4, 9): (9 - 4) / (5 - 0) = 5 / 5 = 1

lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method

In the Gauss-Seidel method, as soon as a new value for a variable is computed, it is used immediately in the current iteration to calculate the subsequent variables. Hence, convergence in Gauss-Seidel is more rapid, and its rate of convergence is roughly twice (
2
2 times) that of the Gauss-Jacobi method.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

9. State the sufficient condition for convergence of Gauss Seidel method

The coefficient matrix must be diagonally dominant. That is, the absolute value of the leading diagonal element in each row is greater than or equal to the sum of the absolute values of the remaining elements in that row:

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?

Lagrange's formula can be used whether the intervals of the independent variable 
𝑥
x are equally spaced or unequally spaced, whereas Newton's formula is applicable only for equal intervals.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Lagrange's formula does not require constructing difference tables and can also be used for inverse interpolation.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

4. Distinguish between direct and indirect method of solving simultaneous equations.   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

Direct method (e.g., Gauss Elimination, Gauss-Jordan): Gives exact solutions in a fixed number of operations.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Indirect / Iterative method (e.g., Gauss-Seidel, Gauss-Jacobi): Gives approximate solutions through successive approximations depending on the accuracy required.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

5. Why Gauss Seidel method is a better method than Jacobi's iterative method.   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

In Gauss-Seidel, the current calculated values of the unknowns are used immediately in the same iteration.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Its rate of convergence is roughly twice that of the Gauss-Jacobi method.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

7. Compare Gauss Elimination and Gauss Jordan methods.   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

Gauss Elimination: Reduces the coefficient matrix to an upper triangular matrix and obtains the solution by back substitution.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Gauss-Jordan: Reduces the coefficient matrix to an identity matrix and gives the solution directly without back substitution.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

3. What advantage has Lagrange's formula over Newton?   
23MA1302-Linear Algebra and Numerical Analysis QB .pdf

It is applicable for both unequally spaced and equally spaced values of 
𝑥
x (Newton requires equal intervals).   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

It does not require difference tables and can be used for inverse interpolation.   
lana unit 1 to 5 class work_compressed.pdf
+ 1

Yes, you can safely skip Question 10.

Anna University Part B uses an Either / Or (Internal Choice) pattern for every unit (e.g., Q13(a) or Q13(b)). In Unit-III (Inner Product Spaces), the syllabus naturally divides into two distinct halves:

First Half (Least Squares & Projections): Questions 6, 7, and 8.

Second Half (Inner Products, Norms, Gram-Schmidt & Inequalities): Questions 9 and 10 (along with Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization).

Because the question paper sets one question from the Least Squares topic and one from the Inner Product / Norm / Orthogonalization topic:

Your Best Path: Focus on Least Squares (Question 8 / Question 7) and Norms & Polynomial Integration (Question 9).

Between Questions 9 and 10, Question 9 is purely numerical—basic integration

Master Questions 7, 8, and 9, and you will have full coverage to score the 16 marks in Unit-III.

Here is the complete strategic breakdown for Unit-IV (Solution of Equations and Eigenvalue Problems) based on Anna University / Panimalar exam patterns.

Part 1: How Many Methods Exist in Unit-IV?

In Unit-IV, all Part-B questions fall into exactly 4 distinct methods, divided cleanly into Two Halves in the question paper:

                  UNIT-IV: EITHER / OR SPLIT
                               │
       ┌───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┐
       ▼                                               ▼
FIRST HALF (Question A)                       SECOND HALF (Question B)
(Algebraic & Linear Systems)                  (Eigenvalue Problems)
1. Newton-Raphson Method                      4. Power Method (Dominant Eigenvalue)
2. Gauss Elimination / Gauss-Jordan              (Finding largest λ & vector X)
3. Gauss-Jacobi / Gauss-Seidel Iteration

Part 2: Question Paper Pattern ("This or That")

Anna University sets Unit-IV Part B as:

𝑄
14
(
𝑎

Q14(a)ORQ14(b)

Q14(a) almost always covers: Newton-Raphson (8 marks) + Gauss-Seidel / Elimination (8 marks).

Q14(b) almost always covers: Power Method (8 or 16 marks) OR Gauss-Jacobi vs Seidel comparison.

Part 3: Which Methods Are Easiest to Learn in Minimum Time?

Ranked by speed of learning, low memorization, and guaranteed marks:

Rank	Method	Why It’s Easy / High Scoring	Risk Level
#1 (Top Pick)	Power Method	Only one single operation: multiply matrix 
A by vector 
X, take the largest number outside, repeat 5–6 times until decimals freeze. No formulas, no determinants, no algebra.	Lowest (Calculator does 100% of the work)
#2 (Top Pick)	Gauss-Seidel Method	Check diagonal dominance, write 
x,y,z formulas, iterate 4–5 times using your calculator. Very mechanical.	Low
#3	Newton-Raphson Method	Find 
f(x) and 
f
′
(x), find an interval 
(a,b) where 
f(a)f(b)<0, iterate formula 3–4 times.	Low-Medium (Watch out for derivative errors)
#4 (Skip if short on time)	Gauss Elimination / Jordan	Matrix row reductions with fractions/decimals. Easy to make an arithmetic sign error in row operations.	Medium
Part 4: Your Winning Strategy

To guarantee the full 16 marks while studying the absolute minimum:

Must Master #1: Power Method

It is virtually guaranteed to appear on one side of the Either/Or choice.

Takes under 15 minutes to learn.

Must Master #2: Gauss-Seidel Iteration

The standard linear system problem that faculty love to set.

Backup / Quick Read: Newton-Raphson

In case Question 14(a) combines Newton-Raphson (8 marks) + Gauss-Seidel (8 marks).

You can safely skip Gauss-Jordan and Gauss Elimination row transformations.

To confirm, which specific question numbers from your Unit-IV question bank sheet should we look at first: the Power Method or Gauss-Seidel?

GROUP 4 (Power Method — Questions 9 & 10) is by far the easiest group.

Here is the exact breakdown of why, followed by how all 4 groups rank from easiest to hardest:

Why Group 4 (Power Method) is the Absolute Easiest:

Zero Formulas to Memorize: You do not need to memorize any complex equations, derivatives, or determinant formulas.

One Repetitive Move: You only do one simple operation over and over:

Matrix 
𝐴
×
Vector 
𝑋
=
New Vector
Matrix A×Vector X=New Vector

Pull out the largest number as 
𝜆
λ, divide the vector by it, and repeat until the numbers freeze.

100% Calculator Work: You can store the matrix in a standard scientific calculator (Casio fx-991MS / ES / EX) and run the matrix multiplication directly. You cannot make an algebraic mistake.

Only 2 Questions to Master: The entire group has only two questions (Q9 and Q10).

Complete Ranking of All 4 Groups (Easiest to Hardest):
Rank	Group	Questions	Why it is at this rank
1 (Easiest)	Group 4: Power Method	Q9, Q10	Pure calculator matrix-vector multiplication. Decimals stabilize in 5 to 7 steps. Highest scoring, zero algebra.
2	Group 3: Iteration (Seidel / Jacobi)	Q6, Q7, Q8	Just write 3 formulas for 
x,y,z. Type them into the calculator formula memory (CALC button) and press = repeatedly until numbers match.
3	Group 1: Newton-Raphson	Q1, Q2, Q3	Easy formula, but: Q1 uses radians (
cosx), Q2 has cubic powers (
x
3
), and Q3 is 
12
	​

. You must remember to set the calculator to Radian mode for Q1, or the entire answer becomes wrong.
4 (Hardest)	Group 2: Direct Solvers (Gauss / Jordan)	Q4, Q5	Row operations with decimals/fractions (

The Verdict:

Start with Group 4 (Power Method: Q9 & Q10) first. It takes 15 minutes to understand both problems, guarantees 8 to 16 marks, and has the lowest risk of calculation errors in the exam hall.

Iteration 7:

$$A X_6 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.500 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1(1) + 6(0.500) + 0 \\ 1(1) + 2(0.500) + 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 4.000 \\ 2.000 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

Taking the largest magnitude element $4$ outside:

$$A X_6 = 4 \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.500 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \lambda_7 X_7 \implies \lambda_7 = 4, \quad X_7 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.500 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

Since $X_6 = X_7 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.5 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$ and $\lambda_6 \approx \lambda_7 = 4$, the values have stabilized. WHY WE GOINT TO 7 INETERAWCT WHY WE STOP ING WAHT IS THEAPIM AND WE FIND THIS OR NOT Iteration 7:

$$A X_6 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.500 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1(1) + 6(0.500) + 0 \\ 1(1) + 2(0.500) + 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 4.000 \\ 2.000 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

Taking the largest magnitude element $4$ outside:

Why We Go to Iteration 7 and Why We Stop There
1. What is the Aim of the Power Method?

The definition of an eigenvalue 
𝜆
λ and an eigenvector 
𝑋
X is:

𝐴
𝑋
=
𝜆
𝑋
AX=λX

This means: multiplying the matrix 
𝐴
A by the vector 
𝑋
X does not change the direction of 
𝑋
X—it only scales 
𝑋
X by the number 
𝜆
λ.

Because we do not know 
𝜆
λ and 
𝑋
X at the start, we make an initial guess 
𝑋
0
X
0

3. Why Do We Stop at Iteration 7? (The Stopping Rule)

In numerical methods and Anna University exam schemes:

The Rule: You stop whenever two consecutive iterations give the same vector and eigenvalue to the required number of decimal places (here, 3 decimal places).

Want to solve Question 10 step-by-step next?
Yes
You said Part 2: Least Latent Root ($\lambda_{\text{least}}$) To find the smallest eigenvalue using the shift…

Part 2: Least Latent Root ($\lambda_{\text{least}}$)

To find the smallest eigenvalue using the shifted power method, form matrix $B = A - \lambda_1 I$:

$$B = A - 4I = \begin{bmatrix} 1-4 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & 2-4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3-4 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}$$

Let initial vector be $Y_0 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$:

Step 1:

$$B Y_0 = \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = -3 \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -0.333 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 2:

$$B Y_1 = \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 6 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -0.333 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -3(1) + 6(-0.333) \\ 1(1) - 2(-0.333) \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -5.0 \\ 1.666 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} = -5 \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -0.333 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

The vector has stabilized at $\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -0.333 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$.

Thus, the dominant eigenvalue of $B$ is $\lambda_B = -5$.

Since $B = A - 4I$:

$$\lambda_B = \lambda_2 - 4 \implies -5 = \lambda_2 - 4 \implies \lambda_2 = \mathbf{-1}$$

Least Latent Root: $\lambda_2 = \mathbf{-1}$

Part 3: The Third Eigenvalue ($\lambda_3$)

By matrix trace property:

$$\text{Sum of all eigenvalues} = \text{Trace}(A)$$

$$\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = a_{11} + a_{22} + a_{33}$$

$$4 + (-1) + \lambda_3 = 1 + 2 + 3$$

$$3 + \lambda_3 = 6 \implies \lambda_3 = 6 - 3 = \mathbf{3}$$

Third Eigenvalue: $\lambda_3 = \mathbf{3}$

Summary of Final Results:

Dominant Eigenvalue: $\lambda_1 = \mathbf{4}$ with Eigenvector $X = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 0.5 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$
Least Latent Root: $\lambda_2 = \mathbf{-1}$
Third Eigenvalue: $\lambda_3 = \mathbf{3}$

Here is the clear explanation of why Part 2 and Part 3 work the way they do, followed by the exact truth about Question 10.

1. Plain English Explanation of Part 2 & Part 3
Why do we need Part 2?

The Power Method (
𝐴
𝑋
=
𝜆
𝑋
AX=λX) has one limitation: it can only find the largest (dominant) number. It cannot directly see small numbers.

To trick the method into finding the smallest number:

Think of a number line where the eigenvalues are 
4

−

By creating matrix

Why is Part 3 so simple?

Plaintext
To find the least latent root:
Let B = A - 4I = [[-3, 6, 1], [1, -2, 0], [0, 0, -1]]
B * [1, 0, 0]^T = [-3, 1, 0]^T = -3 [1, -0.333, 0]^T
B * [1, -0.333, 0]^T = [-5, 1.666, 0]^T = -5 [1, -0.333, 0]^T
Dominant eigenvalue of B is -5.
Least eigenvalue = -5 + 4 = -1.

To find the third eigenvalue:
Trace(A) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
λ1 + λ2 + λ3 = 6
4 + (-1) + λ3 = 6
λ3 = 3.

The Big Rule for Numerical Methods (Unit-IV)

In numerical methods like the Power Method, never round to 2 decimal places. The question specifically demands:

"correct to 3 decimal places"

When you round to 2 decimal places (
0.43
0.43 or 
0.50
0.50), you throw away critical precision, which creates compounding errors in every row-by-column multiplication that follows.

Here is the exact way to round for this exam:

In Anna University Unit-IV, questions are split into an Either / Or choice (16 marks each):

One side almost always carries the Power Method (Q7–Q10).   
image_ff7e06.jpg
+ 1

The other side carries Newton-Raphson (Q1–Q3) or Iterative/Direct Solvers (Gauss-Seidel / Jacobi / Jordan).   
image_ff7e06.jpg

ONLY EASY AND CONFIRM COME TO ECXAM METHOD

In Anna University exams, Unit-IV is split into two choices (Either 11(a) or 11(b), 16 marks each). To guarantee you can answer the paper using only the simplest and fastest steps, focus on these two methods:

Method 1: The Power Method (Guaranteed Full 16 Marks)

This is the easiest method in the syllabus because it uses only basic matrix-vector multiplication with no calculus, row transformations, or linear system pivoting.

Anna University 100-Mark Exam Blueprint (Unit 1 to Unit 5)

Every Anna University theory paper follows the exact same scoring structure:

Part A (10 × 2 = 20 Marks): 2 questions from each unit (Q1–Q10). No choice.

Part B (5 × 16 = 80 Marks): 1 Either/Or question from each unit (Q11–Q15).

Question No.	Unit	Marks	Choice Pattern
Q11 (a) or (b)	Unit 1	16	Either (a) OR (b)
Q12 (a) or (b)	Unit 2	16	Either (a) OR (b)
Q13 (a) or (b)	Unit 3	16	Either (a) OR (b)
Q14 (a) or (b)	Unit 4	16	Either (a) OR (b) — Already covered!
Q15 (a) or (b)	Unit 5	16	Either (a) OR (b)

(To pass comfortably with 50+, you only need 3 full 16-mark questions plus 6–7 two-mark questions. Since Unit 4 is already locked in, you only need 2 more units to cross the pass mark, and all 5 units to score an O / A+ grade.)
